## Isaiah 1:1 (id: 15799)

* *This is the vision*: This term introduces the book as a unified whole, covering Isaiah’s prophecies over about fifty\-five years, from around 740 to 685 BC.
* *Isaiah son of Amoz*: Nothing else is known about Isaiah’s family.
* Jerusalem*,* the capital city of Judah*,* was the center of Isaiah’s ministry. Some of his prophecies also relate to the northern kingdom, Israel (called Samaria and Ephraim, [Isaiah 9:8–21](https://ref.ly/Isa9:8-Isa9:21); [28:1–13](https://ref.ly/Isa28:1-Isa28:13)), which Assyria destroyed in 722 BC (see [2 Kings 17](https://ref.ly/2Kgs17:1-2Kgs17:41)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Have a Vision (ID: `keyterm:HaveAVision`); Isaiah (ID: `person:Isaiah`); Jotham (ID: `person:Jotham.2`); Amoz (ID: `person:Amoz`); Ahaz (ID: `person:Ahaz`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Hezekiah (ID: `person:Hezekiah`); Uzziah (ID: `person:Uzziah`)

## Isaiah 1:2 (id: 15807)

*heavens … earth*: All of creation is called to witness the trial that God has convened against Judah (compare [Deuteronomy 31:28](https://ref.ly/Deut31:28); [32:1](https://ref.ly/Deut32:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Isaiah 1:3 (id: 15808)

*The ox knows its owner*: Even an animal recognizes the one who feeds and cares for it. But Judah did not recognize or obey the Lord in this way.

Even so, despite their rebellion, God still spoke to them as “my people.” This shows his continued grace (undeserved kindness) toward them.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Manger (ID: `realia:Manger`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Perceive (ID: `keyterm:Perceive`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`)

## Isaiah 1:4 (id: 15809)

The term *Holy One of Israel* often appears in Isaiah as a title for the Lord. God is: 

* the righteous and just King of heaven and earth ([Isaiah 6:3–5](https://ref.ly/Isa6:3-Isa6:5)),
* the incomparable God, meaning no one is like him ([Isaiah 40:25](https://ref.ly/Isa40:25)), and
* the Redeemer (the one who rescues and saves) of his people ([Isaiah 43:14](https://ref.ly/Isa43:14)).

Because of this, it was a serious and foolish sin for Judah to reject him.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`)

## Isaiah 1:5 (id: 15810)

God sent famine, war, disease, and death as punishment. However, God often wanted to change stubborn behavior rather than just punish. 

Because of their ongoing rebellion, the people’s “head” is described as injured and their “heart” as sick. This language shows how serious their condition had become.

The nation suffered physical harm from Assyrian attacks. At the same time, they were spiritually defiled and overcome by sin ([Isaiah 64:6](https://ref.ly/Isa64:6)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:5


## Isaiah 1:6 (id: 15811)

*neither cleansed nor bandaged nor soothed with oil*: Judah's national condition was like an injured person who had not received medical care.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`)

## Isaiah 1:7 (id: 15812)

*land … cities*: By 701 BC, the Assyrians had destroyed the infrastructure, captured cities, and devastated the fields (see [Isaiah 64:10](https://ref.ly/Isa64:10)). This fulfilled Isaiah’s prophecy ([Isaiah 6:11–13](https://ref.ly/Isa6:11-Isa6:13)). 

Those who survived the Assyrian attack learned to heed later warnings of exile to Babylon ([Isaiah 39:6–7](https://ref.ly/Isa39:6-Isa39:7)). God fulfills his promises, so his warning of judgment on all nations is certain.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:7


## Isaiah 1:8 (id: 15813)

*the Daughter of Zion*: Zion, one of the hills on which Jerusalem stands, often served as another name for Jerusalem. 

Zion symbolized God's presence ([Psalm 46](https://ref.ly/Ps46:1-Ps46:11)), his protection of his people, and Jerusalem's strength against enemies. Now, however, the opposite is true. Jerusalem has become weak, like a helpless city under siege and like an abandoned shelter or a shack.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Muskmelon (ID: `flora:Muskmelon`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Isaiah 1:9 (id: 15814)

*the LORD of Hosts*: This title refers to the countless angels under the Lord’s command (see [2 Kings 6:16–17](https://ref.ly/2Kgs6:16-2Kgs6:17); [Matthew 26:53](https://ref.ly/Matt26:53)). 

During the exodus, Moses described God as a warrior who fought for his people ([Exodus 15:1–21](https://ref.ly/Exod15:1-Exod15:21)). Ironically, in Isaiah’s time, God fought against Judah because of their wickedness ([Isaiah 1:24](https://ref.ly/Isa1:24); [3:1–5](https://ref.ly/Isa3:1-Isa3:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gomorrah (ID: `place:Gomorrah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`)

## Isaiah 1:10 (id: 15790)

* *Hear the word of the LORD*: Here, *listen* means to obey the law (or *teaching;* Hebrew *torah*) of our God (see [Isaiah 2:3](https://ref.ly/Isa2:3); [5:24](https://ref.ly/Isa5:24)).
* *rulers … people*: All levels of society were guilty.
* Like Sodom and Gomorrah*,* Jerusalem faced divine judgment for rebelling against God’s laws. Jerusalem might have been completely destroyed like those cities if not for God’s grace ([Isaiah 1:9](https://ref.ly/Isa1:9); see [Genesis 19](https://ref.ly/Gen19:1-Gen19:38)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Gomorrah (ID: `place:Gomorrah`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`)

## Isaiah 1:11–15 (id: 15791)

The people had sinned so much that God no longer found joy in their sacrifices. The Lord wanted Judah to stop the religious practices he had commanded them to do rather than to continue them in a manner that was sinful and false ([Isaiah 1:13](https://ref.ly/Isa1:13)). Ceremony cannot replace true godliness.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:11-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** New Moon Festival (ID: `keyterm:NewMoonFestival`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); Mischief (ID: `keyterm:Mischief`); Appointed Feast (ID: `keyterm:AppointedFeast`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Slaughtering (ID: `keyterm:Slaughtering`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Isaiah 1:13 (id: 15792)

* The incense with offerings was meant to be a sweet fragrance to the Lord (see [Exodus 30:7–8](https://ref.ly/Exod30:7-Exod30:8)). However, when offered with hypocrisy, it became a disgusting smell and an offense to God.
* The Lord had designated special fasting days at the temple ([Leviticus 16:31](https://ref.ly/Lev16:31); [23:32](https://ref.ly/Lev23:32); [Numbers 29:7](https://ref.ly/Num29:7)), but Judah's sinfulness ruined them (see [Isaiah 58:1–7](https://ref.ly/Isa58:1-Isa58:7); [Matthew 6:16–18](https://ref.ly/Matt6:16-Matt6:18)).
* *solemn assembly*: These holy days were associated with the new moon and other festivals (see [Leviticus 23](https://ref.ly/Lev23:1-Lev23:44)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); New Moon Festival (ID: `keyterm:NewMoonFestival`); Mischief (ID: `keyterm:Mischief`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`)

## Isaiah 1:14 (id: 15793)

* New Moon celebrations took place on the first day of each lunar month ([Numbers 28:11–15](https://ref.ly/Num28:11-Num28:15)). These days included sacrifices, special meals, and rest from work.
* The annual festivals included:

    + Passover,
        + the Feast of Unleavened Bread,
        + the Feast of Weeks, and
        + the Feast of Tabernacles ([Numbers 28:16–29:39](https://ref.ly/Num28:16-Num29:39); [Deuteronomy 16:1–17](https://ref.ly/Deut16:1-Deut16:17)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** New Moon Festival (ID: `keyterm:NewMoonFestival`); Appointed Feast (ID: `keyterm:AppointedFeast`)

## Isaiah 1:15 (id: 15794)

*I will hide my eyes from you … I will not listen*: The Lord does not answer prayers from those who live in constant wrongdoing. In this situation, God accused the people of twisting his laws to commit violence against innocent victims.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:15


## Isaiah 1:16 (id: 15795)

*Wash and cleanse yourselves*: The people were made unclean by their sin and impure sacrifices. To be clean in God's eyes, they needed to change their behavior, remove their sins, and abandon their evil ways (see also [Isaiah 1:18](https://ref.ly/Isa1:18)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:16


## Isaiah 1:17 (id: 15796)

Seeking justice means following God's standards of fairness and supporting the rights of the oppressed, orphans, and widows—those who are weak and marginalized in society (see also [Jeremiah 7:5–7](https://ref.ly/Jer7:5-Jer7:7); [22:3](https://ref.ly/Jer22:3); [Zechariah 7:10](https://ref.ly/Zech7:10); [Matthew 23:23](https://ref.ly/Matt23:23); [25:31–46](https://ref.ly/Matt25:31-Matt25:46); [James 1:27](https://ref.ly/Jas1:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:17


## Isaiah 1:18 (id: 15797)

* God called his people to come and settle their dispute so that he could restore his covenant relationship with them. Judah had to choose between obedience and rebellion ([Isaiah 1:19–20](https://ref.ly/Isa1:19-Isa1:20)). Isaiah says their sins are "like scarlet" and "red like crimson," the color of blood, which Judah had shed unjustly ([Isaiah 1:15](https://ref.ly/Isa1:15)).
* *white as snow … like wool*: The people would be radically transformed. God would cleanse, purify, and make them holy (see also [Psalm 51:7](https://ref.ly/Ps51:7); [Daniel 7:9](https://ref.ly/Dan7:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Wool (ID: `realia:Wool`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Purple Cloth (ID: `realia:PurpleCloth`)

## Isaiah 1:19–20 (id: 15798)

Repentance leads to life. Resisting God's will and turning away from him leads to death (see [Deuteronomy 11:26–28](https://ref.ly/Deut11:26-Deut11:28); [28:1–62](https://ref.ly/Deut28:1-Deut28:62)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:19-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`)

## Isaiah 1:20 (id: 15800)

*For the mouth of the LORD has spoken*: This statement shows the seriousness of God's promise. The phrase appears 11 times in Isaiah.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`)

## Isaiah 1:21 (id: 15801)

The image of Jerusalem as a prostitute symbolizes Judah's unfaithfulness to God (see [Jeremiah 3:6–14](https://ref.ly/Jer3:6-Jer3:14); [Ezekiel 16:25–26](https://ref.ly/Ezek16:25-Ezek16:26); [Hosea 1–3](https://ref.ly/Hos1:1-Hos3:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Well-Established (ID: `keyterm:BeFirm`)

## Isaiah 1:24 (id: 15802)

*The Lord GOD of Hosts, the Mighty One of Israel**,* was usually Israel’s protector ([Isaiah 31:5](https://ref.ly/Isa31:5)). However, in this context, God was coming to take revenge against his own people. Their wickedness had turned them into his enemies and foes.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Come On! (ID: `keyterm:ComeOn`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Isaiah 1:25 (id: 15803)

* *I will turn My hand against you*: God, who was usually Israel’s protector, would now act against them as a judge. However, his purpose was not only to punish. He planned to correct and restore his people.
* *I will thoroughly purge your dross; I will remove all your impurities.*: God would use the exile in Babylon to remove the wicked from among his people and to refine those who would return.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Hammada (ID: `flora:Soap`)

## Isaiah 1:26 (id: 15804)

The restoration of the community would include new leaders. These leaders would uphold justice instead of twisting it (see [Isaiah 1:23](https://ref.ly/Isa1:23)). 

Jerusalem would once again be like pure silver (see [Isaiah:22](https://ref.ly/Isa1:22)). The city would again be called *the City of Righteousness* and *the Faithful City*.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Well-Established (ID: `keyterm:BeFirm`)

## Isaiah 1:27 (id: 15805)

Here, Zion refers to the remnant (the small group who remain faithful) within the community. Only those who repent (turn away from sin) would receive God’s salvation and cleansing. They would live in his presence.

Zion was meant to be a strong place of justice and righteousness. God loves what is right. He expected the covenant community (the people bound to him by promises) to reflect his holy standards in the way they lived.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Redeem (ID: `keyterm:Redeem`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Isaiah 1:29–30 (id: 15806)

The rebels ([Isaiah 1:28](https://ref.ly/Isa1:28)), who worshipped idols in sacred oak groves and gardens, would become like oaks and gardens suffering from drought. They would miss the life\-giving presence of the true God. Those who followed the Canaanite fertility religion of Baal considered gardens and trees sacred, thinking they brought blessings. Instead, they led to spiritual death.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 1:29-30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Terebinth (ID: `flora:Terebinth`)

## Isaiah 2:1 (id: 15962)

*message* (literally *word*): This heading (see [Isaiah 1:1](https://ref.ly/Isa1:1)) likely introduces the revelation of chapters [2–12](https://ref.ly/Isa2:1-Isa12:6). The main focus is on Judah, but some passages also include: 

* the nations in general ([Isaiah 2:2–4](https://ref.ly/Isa2:2-Isa2:4)),
* all humankind ([Isaiah 2:6–22](https://ref.ly/Isa2:6-Isa2:22)),
* Syria and the northern kingdom of Israel ([Isaiah 7–9](https://ref.ly/Isa7:1-Isa9:21)), and
* the Assyrians ([Isaiah 10](https://ref.ly/Isa10:1-Isa10:34)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Have a Vision (ID: `keyterm:HaveAVision`); Isaiah (ID: `person:Isaiah`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Amoz (ID: `person:Amoz`)

## Isaiah 2:2 (id: 15966)

* In the Old Testament, the phrase *the last days* generally refers to a future time (see [Jeremiah 49:39](https://ref.ly/Jer49:39); [Ezekiel 38:16](https://ref.ly/Ezek38:16); [Hosea 3:5](https://ref.ly/Hos3:5)). In the New Testament, it refers to the period that started with the arrival of Jesus ([Hebrews 1:2](https://ref.ly/Heb1:2)). More specifically, it refers to the time just before the end of the current age ([2 Peter 3:3](https://ref.ly/2Pet3:3)).
* The mountain of the Lord’s house refers to the Temple Mount. This place symbolizes God’s glorious exaltation (see [Isaiah 6:1](https://ref.ly/Isa6:1)) and his kingdom on earth. Isaiah’s focus on God’s supreme kingship comes from his famous vision of God ([Isaiah 6](https://ref.ly/Isa6:1-Isa6:13)).
* Isaiah’s prophetic hope was not a narrow nationalistic dream. It included people from all over the world, beyond Judah and Jerusalem.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Isaiah 2:2–4 (id: 15963)

One day, God's generous salvation will reach beyond Israel and Judah. This will lead nations to come to Zion, not for war, but to receive blessings and live by God's rules. 

Isaiah urged Judah to see the benefits that other nations would gain. He wanted to inspire the people of Judah ([Isaiah 2:5](https://ref.ly/Isa2:5)) to feel motivated and follow the Lord, so they would not miss out on that day. 

This section is almost exactly repeated in [Micah 4:1–3](https://ref.ly/Mic4:1-Mic4:3).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:2-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Mattock (ID: `realia:Mattock`); Pruning Knife (ID: `realia:PruningKnife`); Spear (ID: `realia:Spear`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Isaiah 2:3 (id: 15967)

* Human society will change as people follow God's revelation and his ways.
* Judah had already received God’s teaching and word, but they did not obey it ([Isaiah 1:10–15](https://ref.ly/Isa1:10-Isa1:15)). However, the nations will be ready to do God's will.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Isaiah 2:4 (id: 15968)

* The kingdom of God becomes clear when conflict and violence stop. It is characterized by peace ([Romans 14:17](https://ref.ly/Rom14:17)). People will either choose to cooperate or be compelled to stop fighting ([Psalm 46:9](https://ref.ly/Ps46:9)). Nations will choose divine judgment instead of war (see [Isaiah 1:18–20](https://ref.ly/Isa1:18-Isa1:20)).
* The words *judge* and arbitrate describe God's actions here, but later verses show the Messiah as the one who delivers justice ([Isaiah 11:3–4](https://ref.ly/Isa11:3-Isa11:4)). When this happens, war will end, and nations will turn their weapons into farming tools.
* *swords into plowshares*: Compare [Joel 3:10](https://ref.ly/Joel3:10), where nations are urged to beat their plowshares into swords.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Spear (ID: `realia:Spear`); Pruning Knife (ID: `realia:PruningKnife`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); Mattock (ID: `realia:Mattock`)

## Isaiah 2:5 (id: 15971)

* *Come … let us walk*: Walking in God's light (his revelation) leads to a glorious role in his kingdom (see [Isaiah 60:1](https://ref.ly/Isa60:1); [1 John 1:7](https://ref.ly/1John1:7)). God is the only light that defeats the darkness of sin and evil ([Isaiah 9:2](https://ref.ly/Isa9:2)).

    People cannot create this light on their own ([Isaiah 59:9](https://ref.ly/Isa59:9)). They must walk in the light God provides, trusting him and obeying his will ([Isaiah 50:10](https://ref.ly/Isa50:10)).

* The phrase *house of Jacob* refers to Israel's special historic relationship with the Lord and their history of sin and rebellion (see [Isaiah 14:1](https://ref.ly/Isa14:1); [48:1](https://ref.ly/Isa48:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Isaiah 2:5–22 (id: 15969)

The prophet warned of judgment and mocked human pride. All human systems (religious, economic, military, and social) will face God's examination and be found lacking on the Day of the Lord. This is the final day in history when God will judge the wicked once and for all ([1 Corinthians 1:8](https://ref.ly/1Cor1:8); [1 Thessalonians 5:2](https://ref.ly/1Thess5:2); [2 Thessalonians 2:2](https://ref.ly/2Thess2:2); [2 Peter 3:10](https://ref.ly/2Pet3:10); [Revelation 20:7–15](https://ref.ly/Rev20:7-Rev20:15)). 

Sometimes, prophets used the phrase *the day of the Lord* (or others like it, such as *that day*) to describe specific times when God judged wickedness throughout history (see [Isaiah 13:6](https://ref.ly/Isa13:6), [9](https://ref.ly/Isa13:9); [Ezekiel 13:5](https://ref.ly/Ezek13:5); [30:3](https://ref.ly/Ezek30:3); [Obadiah 1:15](https://ref.ly/Obad1:15)). On the Day of the Lord, only God will be honored. All human wickedness and pride will be defeated. This glorious day of God's arrival will fill wicked and arrogant people with fear ([Isaiah 2:10](https://ref.ly/Isa2:10), [19](https://ref.ly/Isa2:19), [21](https://ref.ly/Isa2:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:5-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Idol (ID: `keyterm:Idol`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Lebanon (ID: `place:Lebanon`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Tarshish (ID: `place:Tarshish`); Mole Rat (ID: `fauna:MoleRat`); Bat (ID: `fauna:Bat`); Ship (ID: `realia:Ship`); Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`); Cedar (ID: `flora:Cedar`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`); Tower (ID: `realia:Tower`); Oak (ID: `flora:Oak`)

## Isaiah 2:5–4.1 (id: 15970)

Isaiah condemned Israel and Judah for their pride and self\-importance. He warned them that only God should be honored. All attempts by humans to lift themselves up will actually result in humiliation.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:5-4:1

## Isaiah 2:6 (id: 15972)

* The Lord had rejected his people to discipline them ([Isaiah 8:17](https://ref.ly/Isa8:17)). Although not permanent, this rejection seriously threatened their hopes for a glorious future.
* *soothsayers*: These were people who practiced pagan religion and magic. God had clearly forbidden this practice in Israel ([Deuteronomy 18:10–11](https://ref.ly/Deut18:10-Deut18:11)).
* The Philistines were ancient enemies of Israel ([Isaiah 9:12](https://ref.ly/Isa9:12); [11:14](https://ref.ly/Isa11:14); [14:29](https://ref.ly/Isa14:29), [31](https://ref.ly/Isa14:31)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`)

## Isaiah 2:7–8 (id: 15973)

Isaiah listed the things people used as substitutes for the true God. These included material wealth and security, such as silver and treasures. They also included military strength, such as warhorses and chariots. People also trusted in idols, which were objects they loved and valued.

This kind of wealth and military power marked the reign of King Uzziah ([2 Chronicles 26:6–21](https://ref.ly/2Chr26:6-2Chr26:21)). However, Isaiah showed that trusting these things was wrong.

In other places, Isaiah explained how foolish it is to worship something made by human hands (see [Isaiah 44:9–20](https://ref.ly/Isa44:9-Isa44:20); [45:16](https://ref.ly/Isa45:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:7-8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Idol (ID: `keyterm:Idol`); Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Isaiah 2:10 (id: 15956)

* On the day of God’s judgment, people will try to hide in caves and among the rocks. These were natural hiding places in the rocky land of Judah. Instead of repenting with humility before the almighty God, they will try to escape his judgment.

    Kings and leaders, as well as slaves and free people, will all look for protection from this judgment(see also [Revelation 6:15](https://ref.ly/Rev6:15)).

* The *terror of the LORD* rrefers to God’s full and powerful wrath against wickedness. This wrath will be revealed on the day of the Lord’s judgment. At this point, the prophecy moves from speaking mainly about Israel ([Isaiah 2:5–6](https://ref.ly/Isa2:5-Isa2:6)) to speaking about all humankind.

    The Lord opposes anything and anyone to speaking about all humankind ([Isaiah 2:11–12](https://ref.ly/Isa2:11-Isa2:12), [22](https://ref.ly/Isa2:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Isaiah 2:11–12 (id: 15957)

* A key theme in Isaiah is that the Lord will be exalted and humble people will be restored ([Isaiah 57:15](https://ref.ly/Isa57:15)), while proud and powerful people are humbled ([26:5](https://ref.ly/Isa26:5)).
* The day of judgment and the day of reckoning are also called *the Day of the LORD*. There are seven references to this in [Isaiah 2–4](https://ref.ly/Isa2:1-Isa4:6) ([2:11](https://ref.ly/Isa2:11), [17](https://ref.ly/Isa2:17), [20](https://ref.ly/Isa2:20); [3:6](https://ref.ly/Isa3:6), [18](https://ref.ly/Isa3:18); [4:1](https://ref.ly/Isa4:1), [2](https://ref.ly/Isa4:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:11-12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Isaiah 2:13 (id: 15959)

* The cedars of Lebanon were valuable trees used to build Solomon’s Temple and palace ([1 Kings 5:6](https://ref.ly/1Kgs5:6)) and other important buildings ([Ezra 3:7](https://ref.ly/Ezra3:7)). The cedars symbolized wealth and power ([2 Chronicles 25:18](https://ref.ly/2Chr25:18); [Psalms 92:12](https://ref.ly/Ps92:12); [104:16](https://ref.ly/Ps104:16)), but these mighty trees were as nothing before the Lord ([Isaiah 10:34](https://ref.ly/Isa10:34); [33:9](https://ref.ly/Isa33:9); see the boast of Sennacherib in [37:24](https://ref.ly/Isa37:24)).
* The oaks of Bashan were also an image of strength and splendor ([Ezekiel 27:6](https://ref.ly/Ezek27:6)). They, too, will disappoint ([Isaiah 33:9](https://ref.ly/Isa33:9); [Nahum 1:4](https://ref.ly/Nah1:4); [Zechariah 11:2](https://ref.ly/Zech11:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Lebanon (ID: `place:Lebanon`); Oak (ID: `flora:Oak`); Cedar (ID: `flora:Cedar`)

*every stately vessel*: This refers to large, decorated sea\-going ships. These ships were a sign of wealth and economic success.

They showed that a nation was strong in trade and rich in resources.

Animals such as rodents and bats live in dark, underground places. They avoid the light and stay hidden.

In the same way, those who worship idols will try to hide from the Lord’s judgment. They will seek dark places instead of turning to God in repentance.

## Isaiah 2:22 (id: 15965)

* The prophecy of judgment in [Isaiah 2:6–21](https://ref.ly/Isa2:6-Isa2:21) between two related calls to action. First, the people are urged to trust the Lord ([Isaiah 2:5](https://ref.ly/Isa2:5)). Then they are warned not to trust in human beings ([Isaiah 2:22](https://ref.ly/Isa2:22)).
* Human strength does not last. It is weak and short\-lived, like a person’s breath (see [Psalm 90](https://ref.ly/Ps90:1-Ps90:17)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 2:22

*remove … food and water*: Long ago, God warned of famine. It could lead to terrible conditions and desperate acts as a judgment for wickedness ([Leviticus 26:26–29](https://ref.ly/Lev26:26-Lev26:29)).

These verses continue discussing judgment against Israel's leaders (see [Isaiah 1:23–25](https://ref.ly/Isa1:23-Isa1:25)). Removing leaders at all levels would let the mob take over. Chaos and famine would turn Judah and Jerusalem into a social wasteland.

## Isaiah 3:2–3 (id: 16141)

On the day of judgment, all military, civil, judicial, and religious leaders will be removed ([Isaiah 39:7](https://ref.ly/Isa39:7); [2 Kings 24:14–16](https://ref.ly/2Kgs24:14-2Kgs24:16)). God will eventually provide good leaders again ([Isaiah 1:26](https://ref.ly/Isa1:26)), but not those who use pagan methods of revelation ([47:9](https://ref.ly/Isa47:9), [12–13](https://ref.ly/Isa47:12-Isa47:13); see [Deuteronomy 18:10–12](https://ref.ly/Deut18:10-Deut18:12)).

## Isaiah 3:4 (id: 16143)

Conditions would be so severe that even boys and toddlers could become candidates for office. Isaiah might have meant that adult leaders would be so reduced in number that only children would be left to take on the role. 

Alternatively, he might have meant that the new leaders would be as immature, unwise, strong\-willed, and inexperienced as children.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 3:4


## Isaiah 3:6–7 (id: 16144)

Things would be so bad (*this heap of rubble*) that no one would want to lead. The people would become so desperate that anyone offering food or clothes could lead them ([Isaiah 3:7](https://ref.ly/Isa3:7)). However, no one would actually have food or clothing (see [Isaiah 4:1](https://ref.ly/Isa4:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 3:6-7

## Isaiah 3:13 (id: 16134)

The Lord is both the prosecutor and the supreme judge in this trial against his people.

A prosecutor is someone who brings charges in court. A judge is the one who decides the case. In this passage, the Lord fills both roles.

## Isaiah 3:14 (id: 16135)

* *elders and leaders*: Wicked and abusive leadership by ungodly and incompetent leaders hinders God's purpose for his people (see also [Isaiah 3:12](https://ref.ly/Isa3:12)). Therefore, leaders face stricter judgment because they are responsible for the well\-being of those they lead.
* *the vineyard*: See the Song of the Vineyard ([Isaiah 5:1–7](https://ref.ly/Isa5:1-Isa5:7)).

## Isaiah 3:15 (id: 16136)

The Lord cares for the poor. These were the marginalized and oppressed (people pushed aside and treated unfairly; see [Isaiah 1:17](https://ref.ly/Isa1:17)). But God is their protector (see [25:4](https://ref.ly/Isa25:4)). 

God asked his people to share his concern for the poor. But instead of helping the poor, many Israelites exploited them to gain wealth (see [Isaiah 3:14](https://ref.ly/Isa3:14)). They used their power to take advantage of those who could not defend themselves.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 3:15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Isaiah 3:17–25 (id: 16139)

*take away their finery*: In a reversal of fortune, the affluent (wealthy) and proud people of Jerusalem would suffer disgrace (public shame). Wealthy women often wore expensive and beautiful clothing or jewelry ([Isaiah 3:18](https://ref.ly/Isa3:18)). 

This *finery* probably included amulets (small objects people believed could protect them from evil). The people wore these items to keep harm away. But these objects could not prevent the disaster of judgment. 

These beautiful items were also status symbols. They showed wealth, importance, and financial security. The people trusted in these outward signs of success.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 3:17-25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Earring (ID: `realia:Earring`); Veil (ID: `realia:Veil`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Turban (ID: `realia:Turban.2`); Tunic (ID: `realia:Tunic.2`); Balsam Tree (ID: `flora:BalsamTree`); Bag (ID: `realia:Bag`); Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`); Sackcloth (ID: `realia:Sackcloth`)

## Isaiah 3:25 (id: 16142)

*Your men will fall … in battle*: Part of the city’s hopeless destitution (extreme poverty and loss) would be the deaths of the men.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 3:25


## Isaiah 4:1 (id: 16283)

During Isaiah’s ministry, Jerusalem’s population was greatly reduced. War, famine (lack of food), and disease caused many deaths ([Isaiah 3:1–3](https://ref.ly/Isa3:1-Isa3:3); [6:13](https://ref.ly/Isa6:13); [14:30](https://ref.ly/Isa14:30); [22:2](https://ref.ly/Isa22:2); [37:4](https://ref.ly/Isa37:4)). 

So many men died that few were left in the city. This created a serious absence of leadership (see [Isaiah 3:1–12](https://ref.ly/Isa3:1-Isa3:12)). Without strong leaders, the city became weak and unstable.

Even worse conditions were coming. In 734–732 BC, Judah faced war with an alliance(a military agreement) between Syria and Israel ([2 Chronicles 28](https://ref.ly/2Chr28:1-2Chr28:27)). Later, from 605 to 586 BC, Babylon conquered Judah. Jerusalem was destroyed and fell to its enemies.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 4:1


## Isaiah 4:2 (id: 16285)

* The *Branch* likely refers to the remnant (the faithful people who remain) who would form Israel’s new beginning after the exile (see [Isaiah 6:13](https://ref.ly/Isa6:13)). Some understand the branch as the ideal descendant of David, the Messiah (God’s chosen king; see [Isaiah 11:1](https://ref.ly/Isa11:1); [Jeremiah 23:5](https://ref.ly/Jer23:5); [33:15–16](https://ref.ly/Jer33:15-Jer33:16); [Zechariah 3:8](https://ref.ly/Zech3:8); [6:12](https://ref.ly/Zech6:12)).
* Isaiah describes a beautiful and glorious change. The people had experienced shame and disgrace ([Isaiah 3:17–25](https://ref.ly/Isa3:17-Isa3:25)). Now they would experience fertility (fruitfulness) and beauty. This change would show God’s presence and blessing among the remnant ([4:6](https://ref.ly/Isa4:6)).
* Even the land would change. The fruit of the land and nature itself would move from desolation (emptiness and ruin) to glory. Isaiah compares this future to the garden of Eden (see [Isaiah 51:3](https://ref.ly/Isa51:3); [65:22](https://ref.ly/Isa65:22)). The message of renewal finds its ultimate fulfillment in the new creation (see [Romans 8:19–27](https://ref.ly/Rom8:19-Rom8:27); [2 Corinthians 5:17](https://ref.ly/2Cor5:17); [Galatians 6:15](https://ref.ly/Gal6:15); [Ephesians 2:15](https://ref.ly/Eph2:15); [4:24](https://ref.ly/Eph4:24); [Revelation 21:22–27](https://ref.ly/Rev21:22-Rev21:27)).
* Those who survive in Israel (the remnant) would be the true citizens of Zion. They would belong to the renewed people of God and share in his promised restoration.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 4:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Wheat (ID: `flora:Wheat`); Sorghum (ID: `flora:Sorghum`)

## Isaiah 4:2–6 (id: 16284)

In bold contrast to the desperate picture in [Isaiah 3:16–4:1](https://ref.ly/Isa3:16-Isa4:1), this prophecy brings salvation and hope. Instead of shame and loss, it promises cleansing, consecration (being set apart for holy use), and renewal. God would cleanse his people from their sin. He would make the remnant holy. He would restore his presence among them.

The imagery in [Isaiah 4:5–6](https://ref.ly/Isa4:5-Isa4:6) alludes to Israel’s exodus from Egypt. At that time, the Lord guided and protected the people with a pillar of cloud by day and a pillar of fire by night. Isaiah uses this picture to describe Israel’s future restoration. Just as God once led his people out of Egypt, he would lead them out of exile. Their return would be like a second exodus.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 4:2-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Refuge (ID: `keyterm:Refuge`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Wheat (ID: `flora:Wheat`); Sorghum (ID: `flora:Sorghum`)

## Isaiah 4:3 (id: 16286)

* To be holy was the requirement for citizenship in Zion. Zion is the place where God dwells in his glorious majesty (see [Isaiah 6:3](https://ref.ly/Isa6:3); [Hebrews 12:14](https://ref.ly/Heb12:14)).
* The remnant would have their names recorded among the living in the Book of Life ([Exodus 32:32](https://ref.ly/Exod32:32); [Daniel 12:1](https://ref.ly/Dan12:1); [Malachi 3:16](https://ref.ly/Mal3:16); [Revelation 3:5](https://ref.ly/Rev3:5); [20:12](https://ref.ly/Rev20:12)).

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`)

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 4:5

## Isaiah 5:1 (id: 16457)

* *my beloved*: This refers to the Lord. The prophet was singing this song on the Lord’s behalf.
* At first, the song seems like a love song. But by [Isaiah 5:3](https://ref.ly/Isa5:3), readers realize it is actually a complaint.
* For more on Israel as God’s vineyard, see also [Isaiah 3:14](https://ref.ly/Isa3:14); [Matthew 21:33–46](https://ref.ly/Matt21:33-Matt21:46); [John 15:1–17](https://ref.ly/John15:1-John15:17).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Isaiah 5:1–7 (id: 16447)

The Song of the Vineyard is a poem where God criticizes the bad leaders who have hurt Israel, his vineyard. Like some wisdom literature, it first describes a confusing situation ([Isaiah 5:1–2](https://ref.ly/Isa5:1-Isa5:2)) and then explains its spiritual meaning ([5:3–7](https://ref.ly/Isa5:3-Isa5:7)). The theme of the vineyard's change appears again in [Isaiah 27](https://ref.ly/Isa27:1-Isa27:13) and in Jesus's teachings ([Matthew 21:33](https://ref.ly/Matt21:33); [Mark 12:1](https://ref.ly/Mark12:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:1-7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Brier (ID: `flora:Brier`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Winepress (ID: `realia:Winepress`); Tower (ID: `realia:Tower`)

## Isaiah 5:1–30 (id: 16446)

The prophet delivered judgment through a song ([Isaiah 5:1–7](https://ref.ly/Isa5:1-Isa5:7)) and prophecies of sorrow ([5:8–30](https://ref.ly/Isa5:8-Isa5:30)). Although Israel's future will be pure and in fellowship with God ([Isaiah 4:2–6](https://ref.ly/Isa4:2-Isa4:6)), it must address the current sinful state of the people and their leaders.

## Isaiah 5:2 (id: 16464)

The Lord cared for his vineyard like an expert farmer. He watched it closely, planting the best vines for sweet grapes. God expected much from Israel as his people (see [Exodus 19:5–6](https://ref.ly/Exod19:5-Exod19:6)), so their bitter grapes (their unrighteous deeds) were useless and disappointing (compare [Galatians 5:22–23](https://ref.ly/Gal5:22-Gal5:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Winepress (ID: `realia:Winepress`); Tower (ID: `realia:Tower`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Isaiah 5:3–4 (id: 16465)

* Clearly, the Lord had kindly done everything possible for the vineyard. He was not responsible for the bitter grapes.
* *I extort you to judge*: Isaiah wanted the audience to condemn the vineyard before revealing that they were the vineyard themselves ([Isaiah 5:7](https://ref.ly/Isa5:7)). This rhetorical tactic is similar to the one Nathan used to confront David ([2 Samuel 12:1–5](https://ref.ly/2Sam12:1-2Sam12:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:3-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Isaiah 5:5–6 (id: 16467)

The Lord promised to turn his vineyard, Israel, into a desolate place because it was only fit for destruction. Later, Isaiah prophesied that Israel would transform from desolation to fruitful vitality (see [Isaiah 55:13](https://ref.ly/Isa55:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:5-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Brier (ID: `flora:Brier`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Isaiah 5:6 (id: 16468)

Sometimes, the lack of rain was seen as a divine judgment ([Deuteronomy 28:23–24](https://ref.ly/Deut28:23-Deut28:24); [Haggai 1:11](https://ref.ly/Hag1:11); [2:16](https://ref.ly/Hag2:16)). This was particularly harmful to a society that relied heavily on farming.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Brier (ID: `flora:Brier`)

## Isaiah 5:7 (id: 16469)

* The identification of Israel as the vineyard is a surprise. It resolves the riddle of the passage.
* Here, a clever play on Hebrew words is used to accuse Israel. The Lord expected justice (Hebrew *mishpat*) but saw only oppression (*mispakh*) of the needy. Instead of righteousness (*tsedaqah*), there were cries of violence (*tse‘aqah*) against the poor (see [Isaiah 1:21–23](https://ref.ly/Isa1:21-Isa1:23)). These similar\-sounding words make the message memorable.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Isaiah 5:8–10 (id: 16470)

*Woe*: The first warning of judgment was against oppressive greed. People took properties, like houses and fields, illegally. The new owners were bullies who used every method to get richer at the expense of the poor (see [1 Kings 21:1–29](https://ref.ly/1Kgs21:1-1Kgs21:29); [Amos 2:6–7](https://ref.ly/Amos2:6-Amos2:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:8-10

## Isaiah 5:8–23 (id: 16471)

This section contains six pronouncements of sorrow. They are six warnings of dreadful judgment ([Isaiah 5:8](https://ref.ly/Isa5:8), [11](https://ref.ly/Isa5:11), [18](https://ref.ly/Isa5:18), [20](https://ref.ly/Isa5:20), [21](https://ref.ly/Isa5:21), [22](https://ref.ly/Isa5:22)). These sorrows highlight some of the "sour grapes" produced by the vineyard of Israel ([5:1–7](https://ref.ly/Isa5:1-Isa5:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:8-23

As with drought ([Isaiah 5:5–6](https://ref.ly/Isa5:5-Isa5:6)), the reduction of crops was at times a divine judgment (see [Haggai 1:6](https://ref.ly/Hag1:6), [9](https://ref.ly/Hag1:9); [2:16](https://ref.ly/Hag2:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:10

## Isaiah 5:11–17 (id: 16449)

*Woe*: The second threat of judgment concerns indulgent lifestyles (lives centered on pleasure and excess). The language in this section shows a life of moral corruption. The people live for pleasure while ignoring justice and righteousness.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:11-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Hell (ID: `place:Hell`); Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`); Intention (ID: `keyterm:Intention`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Drum (ID: `realia:Drum`); Flute (ID: `realia:Flute`); Lyre (ID: `realia:Lyre`); Lute (ID: `realia:Lute`)

## Isaiah 5:12 (id: 16450)

These people are self\-indulgent and content. They do not think about the Lord. They are too busy enjoying their drunken parties to consider why things went wrong. They do not reflect on why God sent no rain or why they lost a battle.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Drum (ID: `realia:Drum`); Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`); Flute (ID: `realia:Flute`); Lyre (ID: `realia:Lyre`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Lute (ID: `realia:Lute`)

## Isaiah 5:13 (id: 16451)

* Israel and Judah would go into exile to Assyria in 722 BC and to Babylon in 586 BC.
* The people did not respond to the Lord because they did not truly know him (see [Isaiah 1:3](https://ref.ly/Isa1:3)). They likely knew about him, but their behavior showed that they did not have a close relationship with him.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Intention (ID: `keyterm:Intention`)

## Isaiah 5:14 (id: 16452)

The grave (Hebrew *Sheol*) represented the place of the dead in ancient Near Eastern thinking (see [Isaiah 14:9](https://ref.ly/Isa14:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`); Hell (ID: `place:Hell`)

## Isaiah 5:16 (id: 16453)

God's rule is always fair and right, and he will be honored for his justice. When he is honored, oppressors will face judgment (see [Isaiah 1:21–23](https://ref.ly/Isa1:21-Isa1:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`)

## Isaiah 5:18 (id: 16455)

God's ropes were "cords of kindness, with ropes of love" for Israel ([Hosea 11:4](https://ref.ly/Hos11:4)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:18

## Isaiah 5:18–19 (id: 16454)

*Woe*: The third threat of judgment was against those who mocked and openly disrespected God's authority.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:18-19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Counsel (ID: `keyterm:Counsel`); Wagon (ID: `realia:Wagon`); Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`)

## Isaiah 5:19 (id: 16456)

* The people challenged God to hurry and act. They wanted him to prove that he truly exists and rules the world.

    The verb *hurry* translates two Hebrew verbs:

    + *maher* (“be quick”)
        + *khush* (“be speedy”)
        These same words appear in the name *Maher\-shalal\-hash\-baz* ([Isaiah 8:1](https://ref.ly/Isa8:1)).

* God had revealed his plan to Isaiah. The people did not understand that God’s purposes would turn against them. Instead of bringing the kind of help they expected, God’s action would bring judgment.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Counsel (ID: `keyterm:Counsel`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`)

## Isaiah 5:20 (id: 16458)

* *Woe*: The fourth threat of judgment was against a lack of integrity.
* *evil good … darkness to light … sweet with bitter*: This distorted view of life corrupts God's holy order.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`)

## Isaiah 5:21 (id: 16459)

*Woe*: The fifth threat of judgment was about the folly (foolishness) of self\-deception. Self\-deception means lying to oneself. The people believed they were wise and clever. They trusted their own understanding instead of trusting the Lord.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); Perceive (ID: `keyterm:Perceive`)

## Isaiah 5:22–24 (id: 16460)

*Woe*: The sixth threat of judgment was against the indulgent lifestyle of the wicked.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:22-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`); Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Isaiah 5:24 (id: 16461)

* The image of rotting roots reflects human transience (the short and fragile nature of human life; see [Isaiah 11:1](https://ref.ly/Isa11:1); [27:6](https://ref.ly/Isa27:6); [37:31](https://ref.ly/Isa37:31)).
* To despise the Lord’s word means to reject or ignore it. It means living without concern for obeying what God has said.

    This attitude leads to folly (foolish living) and ruin ([Proverbs 1:30](https://ref.ly/Prov1:30); [5:12](https://ref.ly/Prov5:12); [15:5](https://ref.ly/Prov15:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Isaiah 5:25 (id: 16462)

*mountains quake*: This describes God’s wrath against sinners. The prophets often express God’s anger as the shaking of the earth’s foundations ([Isaiah 13:13](https://ref.ly/Isa13:13); [23:11](https://ref.ly/Isa23:11); [24:18–19](https://ref.ly/Isa24:18-Isa24:19); [29:6](https://ref.ly/Isa29:6); [54:10](https://ref.ly/Isa54:10)). God’s appearance in judgment or salvation through natural events is called a *theophany* (a visible display of God's presence). In these moments, God reveals himself through smoke, darkness, lightning, or earthquake (see [Isaiah 6:1–4](https://ref.ly/Isa6:1-Isa6:4), [6](https://ref.ly/Isa6:6); [13:13](https://ref.ly/Isa13:13); [Exodus 19:18–19](https://ref.ly/Exod19:18-Exod19:19); [Psalm 18:7–15](https://ref.ly/Ps18:7-Ps18:15); [Micah 1:3–4](https://ref.ly/Mic1:3-Mic1:4); [Revelation 8:5](https://ref.ly/Rev8:5); [16:17–18](https://ref.ly/Rev16:17-Rev16:18); [18:21–22](https://ref.ly/Rev18:21-Rev18:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Isaiah 5:26 (id: 16463)

* The signal was a *banner* raised on a long pole. It was often placed on a high hill so people could see it from far away (see also [Isaiah 13:2](https://ref.ly/Isa13:2); [18:3](https://ref.ly/Isa18:3); [30:17](https://ref.ly/Isa30:17)).

    In Isaiah, the phrase *a* *banner for the distant nations* appears many times. Sometimes it calls nations to join in battle, as in this passage. In other places, it calls them to share in salvation ([Isaiah 11:10](https://ref.ly/Isa11:10), [12](https://ref.ly/Isa11:12); [49:22](https://ref.ly/Isa49:22); [62:10](https://ref.ly/Isa62:10)).

* The *distant nations* here refer to the Assyrians and Babylonians. God used them as instruments (tools) of judgment. Assyria conquered Israel in 722 BC. Babylon destroyed Judah and Jerusalem in 586 BC.

    Isaiah describes them as wild animals called in to trample the vineyard ([Isaiah](https://ref.ly/Isa5:5) [5:1–7](https://ref.ly/Isa5:1-Isa5:7)). They would fiercely attack Israel and Judah until God's judgment was complete ([Isaiah 5:27–30](https://ref.ly/Isa5:27-Isa5:30)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Standard (ID: `realia:Standard`)

## Isaiah 5:30 (id: 16466)

* *that day*: see study note on Isaiah 2:5–22.

* In Old Testament prophecy, darkness often represents God’s alienation (separation), wrath, and judgment (see also [Isaiah 8:22](https://ref.ly/Isa8:22)). By contrast, light stands for God’s holy presence and blessing (see especially [Isaiah 60:2](https://ref.ly/Isa60:2), [19](https://ref.ly/Isa60:19)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 5:30
* **Associated Articles:** Isaiah 2:5-22 (ID: `15969@TyndaleStudyNotes`)


## Isaiah 6:1 (id: 16622)

* When King Uzziah died, the Assyrians began to regain control in the ancient Near East. This started a period dominated by major world powers:

    + Assyria,
        + Babylonia,
        + Persia,
        + Greece, and
        + Rome.
* *I saw the Lord*: The term *Lord* (Hebrew *’adonay*) can refer to human rulers or to God. God is the ultimate King over all nations and rulers.
* *on a throne*: The apostle John also had a vision of God's heavenly throne (see [Revelation 4:2](https://ref.ly/Rev4:2); [7:10](https://ref.ly/Rev7:10); [21:5](https://ref.ly/Rev21:5)).
* Isaiah says that the train (the flowing edge) of the Lord’s robe filled the temple. If only the train of his robe filled the temple, this suggests how great and majestic the Lord himself must be. The robe is only a small part of what Isaiah sees. Yet even that part fills the sacred space.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 6:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Uzziah (ID: `person:Uzziah`); Train (ID: `realia:Train`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Throne (ID: `realia:Throne`)

## Isaiah 6:1–13 (id: 16618)

Isaiah’s great vision of the Lord as King on his throne was a turning point in his life. This vision shaped his work as a prophet.

Scholars understand the historical setting of this event in two main ways:

1. Some see [Isaiah 6](https://ref.ly/Isa6:1-Isa6:13) as Isaiah's original call to ministry. This would mean [Isaiah 1–5](https://ref.ly/Isa1:1-Isa5:30) and [7–12](https://ref.ly/Isa7:1-Isa12:6) belong to the time of King Ahaz. Ahaz began to rule after King Uzziah died([Isaiah 6:1](https://ref.ly/Isa6:1)).

    If this is correct, Isaiah’s call did not appear at the beginning of the book for literary reasons. Perhaps the author placed the call in [chapter 6](https://ref.ly/Isa6:1-Isa6:13) to end [chapters 1–5](https://ref.ly/Isa1:1-Isa5:30) and introduce [chapters 7–12](https://ref.ly/Isa7:1-Isa12:6).

    Isaiah’s personal cleansing and commissioning show what the whole nation needed. The people also needed to turn back to God if they were to fulfill their calling to be God’s light to the world.

2. Others believe that [chapters 1–12](https://ref.ly/Isa1:1-Isa12:6) follow a chronological order (in the order events happened).

    In this view, chapter 6 does not describe Isaiah’s first call. Instead, it describes a recommissioning. This means God sent Isaiah again to serve in a new way.

    This new stage of ministry took place during the time of Ahaz. During this period, people would reject Isaiah’s message.

    Supporters of this interpretation refer to [Isaiah 2:7–9](https://ref.ly/Isa2:7-Isa2:9); [3:16–24](https://ref.ly/Isa3:16-Isa3:24); and [5:8–14](https://ref.ly/Isa5:8-Isa5:14). They argue that these sections describe a time of wealth, military strength, pride, and luxury. These conditions fit the reign of Uzziah but not the reign of Ahaz.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 6:1-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Seraph (ID: `deity:Seraph`); Perceive (ID: `keyterm:Perceive`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Uzziah (ID: `person:Uzziah`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe`); Train (ID: `realia:Train`); Doorstep (ID: `realia:Doorstep`); Terebinth (ID: `flora:Terebinth`); Temple Altar (ID: `realia:TempleAltar`); Oak (ID: `flora:Oak`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Tabernacle Altar (ID: `realia:TabernacleAltar`); Throne (ID: `realia:Throne`); Altar (ID: `realia:Altar`)

## Isaiah 6:2 (id: 16623)

The mighty seraphim are heavenly beings not mentioned elsewhere in Scripture (though similar living creatures appear in [Revelation 4:6–9](https://ref.ly/Rev4:6-Rev4:9); [15:8](https://ref.ly/Rev15:8)). The Hebrew term *seraphim* may be related to a word that means “to burn.” This suggests an association with fire. Their name may point to their shining or fiery nature.

In Isaiah’s vision, each seraph had six wings. With two wings they covered their faces. This showed reverence and humility. Even these powerful heavenly beings did not look directly at God’s glory.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 6:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Seraph (ID: `deity:Seraph`)

## Isaiah 6:3 (id: 16624)

* The repeated use of the word *holy* shows that the Lord is completely set apart from all creation.
* *all the earth is full*: The Hebrew text does not include a verb in this line. Because of this, translators understand the seraphim’s words in two possible ways.

    1. The earth may already be filled with God’s glory through creation, even though sin has damaged it.
        2. The earth may be filled with the Lord’s glory in the future, when he fully establishes his rule as King over the earth.
        Both understandings point to the same truth. One day, the Lord’s glorious presence will be known more fully on earth. This will happen even though humankind is sinful and wicked nations are powerful.

    God also revealed His glory in a greater way through the incarnation, when the Son of God became human ([John 12:41](https://ref.ly/John12:41); [2 Corinthians 3:18](https://ref.ly/2Cor3:18); [4:4–7](https://ref.ly/2Cor4:4-2Cor4:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 6:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`)

## Isaiah 6:5 (id: 16625)

*Woe is me, for I am ruined*: The prophet felt unworthy of the vision. He was painfully aware of his personal uncleanness (*unclean lips*) when compared to the holiness of God. Isaiah knew that he was unworthy to speak the pure word of God, as were his people.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 6:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`)

## Isaiah 6:7 (id: 16626)

*your iniquity is removed and your sin is atoned for*: The prophet's experience of forgiveness anticipated Israel's need for national forgiveness and cleansing ([Isaiah 43:25](https://ref.ly/Isa43:25); [44:22](https://ref.ly/Isa44:22); see study note on Isaiah 39:6–7).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 6:7
* **Associated Articles:** Isaiah 39:6-7 (ID: `16282@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`)

## Isaiah 6:8 (id: 16627)

* *Who will go for Us?*: The Lord spoke in the presence of his angels. The Bible sometimes describes this gathering as a heavenly council (a group of heavenly beings who stand before God; see also [1 Kings 22:19–22](https://ref.ly/1Kgs22:19-1Kgs22:22); [Jeremiah 23:18](https://ref.ly/Jer23:18), [22](https://ref.ly/Jer23:22)).
* *Here am I*: Isaiah answered quickly and willingly. God had just forgiven and cleansed him from sin. Because of this grace (God’s undeserved kindness), Isaiah was ready to serve.

    He committed himself to a lifetime of ministry (service for God).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 6:8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Isaiah 6:9 (id: 16628)

*never understanding … never perceiving*: The prophet's words revealed the true feelings of his audience. The people were so deep in sin that hearing the truth pushed them further from God instead of closer (for example, Ahaz, [Isaiah 7](https://ref.ly/Isa7:1-Isa7:25)). Yet, the truth needed to be spoken. 

Jesus also used this principle in his own preaching ([Matthew 13:14–15](https://ref.ly/Matt13:14-Matt13:15); [Luke 8:10](https://ref.ly/Luke8:10); [19:42](https://ref.ly/Luke19:42); [John 12:40](https://ref.ly/John12:40); see also [Acts 28:26–27](https://ref.ly/Acts28:26-Acts28:27); [Romans 11:8](https://ref.ly/Rom11:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 6:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Perceive (ID: `keyterm:Perceive`)

## Isaiah 6:10 (id: 16619)

* Isaiah was God’s messenger. Through his message, the wicked became more blind and deaf (they refused to see and hear the truth). But those who honored God gained spiritual sight and hearing (they understood and obeyed; see also [Isaiah 29:9–10](https://ref.ly/Isa29:9-Isa29:10), [18](https://ref.ly/Isa29:18); [35:5](https://ref.ly/Isa35:5); [42:7](https://ref.ly/Isa42:7), [16–19](https://ref.ly/Isa42:16-Isa42:19); [43:8](https://ref.ly/Isa43:8); [56:10](https://ref.ly/Isa56:10); [59:10](https://ref.ly/Isa59:10)).
* *turn and be healed*: The Lord was ready to heal those who truly repented, but not those who only wanted to avoid judgment while continuing their sinful ways.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 6:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Perceive (ID: `keyterm:Perceive`)

## Isaiah 6:11 (id: 16620)

*How long … until … the land is desolate and ravaged*: During the prophet's ministry (740 to 685 BC), wars, famines, and devastation plagued the nation.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 6:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Isaiah 6:13 (id: 16621)

* Even if *a tenth*, a remnant, survives, the judgment would continue until almost all are destroyed. Isaiah often spoke about near destruction or decimation (almost complete destruction; [Isaiah 9:18–21](https://ref.ly/Isa9:18-Isa9:21); [10:20](https://ref.ly/Isa10:20); [22:4](https://ref.ly/Isa22:4); [48:19](https://ref.ly/Isa48:19); [51:18](https://ref.ly/Isa51:18)). But Isaiah also kept hope alive. A remnant would survive and begin again ([7:21–22](https://ref.ly/Isa7:21-Isa7:22); [29:23](https://ref.ly/Isa29:23); [37:31](https://ref.ly/Isa37:31); [44:26](https://ref.ly/Isa44:26); [49:19](https://ref.ly/Isa49:19); [54:1](https://ref.ly/Isa54:1)).
* *the holy seed will be a stump in the land*: The stump is like the base of a tree that remains after it is cut down. It represents a remnant of holy people (see [Isaiah 4:2–3](https://ref.ly/Isa4:2-Isa4:3)). From this small beginning, a new leader would come who pleased the Lord ([11:1](https://ref.ly/Isa11:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 6:13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Terebinth (ID: `flora:Terebinth`); Oak (ID: `flora:Oak`)

## Isaiah 7:1 (id: 16729)

* Rezin was king of Syria. Damascus was Syria’s capital city.
* Pekah was the king of Israel from 740 to 732 BC. He was a famous warrior ([2 Chronicles 28:5–8](https://ref.ly/2Chr28:5-2Chr28:8)).
* Pekah and Rezin began to attack Jerusalem while Jotham was king, from 750 to 732 BC. They increased their attacks during the early years of young King Ahaz ([2 Kings 15:37](https://ref.ly/2Kgs15:37); [16:5](https://ref.ly/2Kgs16:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 7:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ahaz (ID: `person:Ahaz`); Rezin (ID: `person:Rezin`); Remaliah (ID: `person:Remaliah`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Jotham (ID: `person:Jotham.2`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Pekah (ID: `person:Pekah`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Uzziah (ID: `person:Uzziah`)

## Isaiah 7:1–25 (id: 16720)

At one point in his reign, Ahaz faced a serious crisis. The kings of Syria and Israel attacked Judah. They planned to remove Ahaz and force Judah to join them in resisting Assyria.

Instead of trusting the Lord, Ahaz asked Assyria for help ([2 Kings 16:7–10](https://ref.ly/2Kgs16:7-2Kgs16:10)). By doing this, he rejected Isaiah’s call to trust in God ([Isaiah 7:12](https://ref.ly/Isa7:12)). 

The Assyrians defeated Syria and Israel. In time, both nations fell. However, Assyria later turned against Judah and sought to control it completely.

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 7:1-25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Ephraim (ID: `person:Ephraim`); Tribe of Ephraim (ID: `group:Ephraim`); Remaliah (ID: `person:Remaliah`); Ahaz (ID: `person:Ahaz`); Brier (ID: `flora:Brier`); Rezin (ID: `person:Rezin`); Assyria (ID: `place:Assyria`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); David (ID: `person:David`); Shear-jashub (ID: `person:Shear-jashub`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Well-Established (ID: `keyterm:BeFirm`); Launderer’s Field (ID: `place:WashersField`); Isaiah (ID: `person:Isaiah`); Tabeal (ID: `person:Tabeal`); Damascus (ID: `place:Damascus`); Jotham (ID: `person:Jotham.2`); Hell (ID: `place:Hell`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Pekah (ID: `person:Pekah`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Arrow (ID: `keyterm:Arrow`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Believe In (ID: `keyterm:BelieveIn`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Uzziah (ID: `person:Uzziah`); Euphrates River (ID: `place:Euphrates`); Hoe (ID: `realia:Hoe`); Fly (ID: `fauna:Fly`); Razor (ID: `realia:Razor`); Molten Image (ID: `realia:MoltenImage`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Arrow (ID: `realia:Arrow`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Bow (ID: `realia:Bow`); Bee (ID: `fauna:Bee`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`)

## Isaiah 7:1–12.6 (id: 16719)

These prophecies occurred when Assyria rose to power. Syria and Israel formed an alliance against Judah ([Isaiah 7:1–2](https://ref.ly/Isa7:1-Isa7:2); see [2 Kings 16:5](https://ref.ly/2Kgs16:5)). God used Assyria as a tool to punish Syria, Israel, and Judah ([Isaiah 7–10](https://ref.ly/Isa7:1-Isa10:34)).

* **Associated Passages:** Isaiah 7:1-12:6

## Isaiah 7:1–39.8 (id: 16721)

In this long section of the book, the nation of Israel was confronted with a vision of God, similar to how Isaiah was confronted in [chapter 6](https://ref.ly/Isa6:1-Isa6:13).
